<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The page 8 layout hasn't changed — Figure 7 is still in the left column below Table 2, not in the right column. The move didn't work because LaTeX still encounters both floats before it has enough text to fill the left column.

The problem is that in two-column mode, LaTeX processes floats as it encounters them in the source. Both Table 2 and Figure 7 are [t] floats. When LaTeX starts page 8, it places Table 2 at left-col top. Then it encounters Figure 7 and since there's room below Table 2 in the left column (or the right column hasn't started yet), it places Figure 7 there too.


I'm realizing the real issue: the [t] specifier only means "top of column," not "top of right column." LaTeX fills the left column first with floats in source order, and the second float only moves to the right column once the left column is full. I need to insert enough body text between Table 2 and Figure 7 to force the left column to fill up before Figure 7 is processed. The most straightforward solution is probably to move Figure 7 into the Discussion section entirely — by then there's enough accumulated text that the left column will be full when the figure floats, naturally pushing it to the right column where I want it. Let me try that approach.
```

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	\emph{What is the practical overhead?}
6	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency (Appendix~\ref{app:ft-latency}).
7	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
10	\label{sec:beam-results}
11	% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep
12	\emph{Can beam search mitigate CF violations?}
13	With LoRA, beam search~\cite{anderson2017guided} achieves near-zero violations at 8B on both SciERC and DocRED (DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\% for Llama-3.1-8B; $\leq$0.01\% residual CF for Qwen3-8B); at 70B, beam $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\% with one residual CF on SciERC (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
14	A beam-width sweep ($k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$) reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\%, at 1.18$\times$ greedy S1 latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\% at 1.39$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:beam-pareto}; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
15	This yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\%, DR${\leq}$0.21\%, CF${\leq}$1.4\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\times$, near-zero violations at 8B).
16	
17	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
18	%  Table: Fine-tuning composability (moved here for page 8 placement)
19	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\small

35	  & & S1    & \textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\downarrow$100\% \\

37	  & \multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}
38	    & Unconstr.\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\

51	\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\times$ unconstrained vs.\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).
52	F1 (\%). No $\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$; power analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:power-analysis-full}).
53	E-F1 (entity F1) includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF (parse failure): 7--8\%; Llama PF: 2--3\%).}

56	
57	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
58	\paragraph{70B-class validation.}
59	\label{sec:70b-results}
60	\emph{Does the layer interaction change with model scale?}
61	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\%, DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\%); semantic-only achieves 84\% DR reduction vs.\ 8B's 24\% (Figure~\ref{fig:scale-dependency}).
62	LoRA confirms composability: DR\,$\to$\,0\% on both datasets (R-F1 44--45; Appendices~\ref{app:70b-ablation},~\ref{app:70b-docred-zs},~\ref{app:70b-docred},~\ref{app:quantization}).
63

70	\caption{DR reduction (\%) across 3 models $\times$ 2 datasets (zero-shot S1). Calibrated models (Qwen, Llama) achieve $\geq$90\% on SciERC; DocRED's higher $\bar{\kappa}$ reduces effectiveness. Full violation breakdown in Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).}
71	\label{fig:crossmodel-heatmap}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
75	\paragraph{GCD comparison.}
76	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
77	Our grammar-only row (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}) is analytically equivalent to GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}/BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \texttt{guided\_json}~\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms this (DR${=}$15.26\%; Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
78	The ${\sim}$15\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\% total reduction).
79

    "originalFile": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\n\\emph{What is the practical overhead?}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-latency}).\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\n% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep\n\\emph{Can beam search mitigate CF violations?}\nWith LoRA, beam search~\\cite{anderson2017guided} achieves near-zero violations at 8B on both SciERC and DocRED (DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\\% for Llama-3.1-8B; $\\leq$0.01\\% residual CF for Qwen3-8B); at 70B, beam $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\\% with one residual CF on SciERC (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nA beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, at 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% at 1.39$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:beam-pareto}; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nThis yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\%, DR${\\leq}$0.21\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\\times$, near-zero violations at 8B).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: Fine-tuning composability (moved here for page 8 placement)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Soopb4CWv3sWt8C3WL7GmA`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	Neither layer alone suffices: grammar-only achieves 3\% DR reduction, semantic-only 24\%, but their composition yields 94\%, an enabling dependency that weakens at scale (84\% at 70B).
4	This non-additive interaction arises because the grammar layer provides a structured prerequisite: by restricting relation tokens to $\mathcal{R}$ and entity type tokens to $\mathcal{E}$, it ensures the semantic layer receives well-formed inputs on which domain/range lookups can operate.
5	Without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership, the semantic layer cannot reliably identify which relation governs a given type position.
6	
7	OntoCoDec composes both layers, reducing DR to $\leq$\,0.21\% across model families and datasets ($\pm$0.3\,pp R-F1).
8	The residual DR$\to$CF conversion is a severity downgrade rather than an error introduction: DR violations produce ontology-invalid triples that corrupt downstream knowledge graphs and require re-extraction, while CF violations (cross-triple type inconsistency) preserve individually valid triples and are resolvable via beam search or post-hoc reranking.
9	Beam $k{=}4$ achieves near-zero violations at 8B, making generative IE reliably schema-compliant.
10	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nNeither layer alone suffices:",
    "newString": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Figure: Cross-Model Heatmap (placed here for right-column [t] on p8)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_crossmodel_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{DR reduction (\\%) across 3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets (zero-shot S1). Calibrated models (Qwen, Llama) achieve $\\geq$90\\% on SciERC; DocRED's higher $\\bar{\\kappa}$ reduces effectiveness. Full violation breakdown in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model} (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).}\n\\label{fig:crossmodel-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nNeither layer alone suffices:",
    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nNeither layer alone suffices: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%, an enabling dependency that weakens at scale (84\\% at 70B).\nThis non-additive interaction arises because the grammar layer provides a structured prerequisite: by restricting relation tokens to $\\mathcal{R}$ and entity type tokens to $\\mathcal{E}$, it ensures the semantic layer receives well-formed inputs on which domain/range lookups can operate.\nWithout grammar-enforced vocabulary membership, the semantic layer cannot reliably identify which relation governs a given type position.\n\nOntoCoDec composes both layers, reducing DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across model families and datasets ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp R-F1).\nThe residual DR$\\to$CF conversion is a severity downgrade rather than an error introduction: DR violations produce ontology-invalid triples that corrupt downstream knowledge graphs and require re-extraction, while CF violations (cross-triple type inconsistency) preserve individually valid triples and are resolvable via beam search or post-hoc reranking.\nBeam $k{=}4$ achieves near-zero violations at 8B, making generative IE reliably schema-compliant.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 15,
        "lines": [
          " % Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion",

          "+\\centering",
          "+\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_crossmodel_heatmap.pdf}",
          "+\\caption{DR reduction (\\%) across 3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets (zero-shot S1). Calibrated models (Qwen, Llama) achieve $\\geq$90\\% on SciERC; DocRED's higher $\\bar{\\kappa}$ reduces effectiveness. Full violation breakdown in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model} (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).}",
          "+\\label{fig:crossmodel-heatmap}",

          " This non-additive interaction arises because the grammar layer provides a structured prerequisite: by restricting relation tokens to $\\mathcal{R}$ and entity type tokens to $\\mathcal{E}$, it ensures the semantic layer receives well-formed inputs on which domain/range lookups can operate.",
          " Without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership, the semantic layer cannot reliably identify which relation governs a given type position."

  "content": "[飞书用户 用户]: 📊 论文自检任务（重大）：全文数据真实性与可追溯性彻底审计\n\n这是一次彻底的大检查，必须认真对待。AI 写论文最致命的两个问题：编数字（hallucinate）和抄数字（数字之间不一致或来源不明）。这次的目标是把每一个出现在论文里的数字都\"对账\"清楚。\n\n⚠️ 凡是查不到出处的数字，要么找到出处，要么删掉。不允许凭印象、凭\"差不多\"、凭老版本残留的数字留在论文里。\n\n---\n\n审计范围（无遗漏）：\n\n1. 图（Figures）\n   - 每张图里的数据点、bar 高度、曲线值、error bar\n   - 图的 caption 里出现的数字（\"reduces by 23%\"、\"on average 4.2\"）\n   - 坐标轴 tick 标注\n\n2. 表（Tables）\n   - 主结果表、ablation 表、超参表、基线表\n   - 单元格里的每个数字，包括精度（小数位数）\n   - 表头里的尺寸说明（\"N=1000\"、\"k=5\"）\n\n3. 正文（Body）\n   - Abstract 里的关键 claim 数字\n   - Introduction 的 contribution 数字\n   - Method 描述里的超参（learning rate、batch、steps、模型尺寸、参数量）\n   - Experimental setup（数据集大小、训练 GPU 小时数、wall-clock 时间）\n   - Analysis 段落里逐一引用的数字\n   - Conclusion 里复述的数字\n\n4. 附录（Appendix）\n   - 完整的超参表、额外实验结果\n   - 数据集详细统计\n   - 失败案例的统计\n   - Proof 里的常数、bound\n\n---\n\n对每个数字，逐个确认这 3 个问题：\n\nQ1（真实性）：这个数字是从一次 真实运行 的实验里出来的吗？\n\n合格证据：\n- 在 results.yaml / 实验日志 / WandB run 里能定位到原始记录\n- registry 里有对应的实验 ID\n- 知道是 seed N 跑的、还是 mean of N seeds\n\n不合格：\n❌ \"之前跑过一版差不多是这个数\"\n❌ \"我们估算的\"（除非论文明确说明 \"estimated\"）\n❌ \"上次 AI 写的时候放进来的，我也不知道哪来的\"\n❌ \"占位符忘删了\"\n\nQ2（正确性）：抄到论文里有没有抄错？\n\n- 原始记录 73.18% 抄到表里有没有变成 73.81% 或 73.2%？\n- 小数位精度跟原始一致吗？（不要四舍五入到误差范围以外）\n- 单位对吗（accuracy vs F1，pp vs %，BLEU vs ROUGE）\n\nQ3（可追溯性）：数字写在论文里几个地方，每个地方都一致吗？\n\n同一个指标可能出现在：Abstract、Introduction contribution、Method、Main Table、Analysis 段落、Conclusion —— 必须逐字逐位一致。不允许 Abstract 说 73.2%、表里 73.18%、Conclusion 又变成 73%。\n\n---\n\n审计步骤（请按这个顺序）：\n\n1. 列清单：把论文里出现的所有数字（图+表+正文+附录）列出来。Director 自己估算一下总共多少个数字（一般 200-500 个）。\n\n2. 逐个对账：每个数字找 results.yaml / wandb / 日志 / registry 里的源头。能找到就在自检表里记一行 \"数字 X → 来源 Y\"。\n\n3. 不一致清单：列出 (a) 找不到出处的，(b) 跟源头对不上的，(c) 论文内部前后不一致的。\n\n4. 决策：每个不一致项决定怎么修：\n   - 能找到出处 → 改成正确的，并保证全文一致\n   - 实在找不到出处 → 删掉，不要硬撑\n\n5. 二次扫描：修完后再过一遍正文，确认没有遗留。\n\n---\n\n汇报内容（完成后）：\n\n(a) 总共审计了多少个数字？\n(b) 发现多少个\"不一致 / 找不到出处\"的问题？分别在哪些章节？\n(c) 这些问题分别是怎么处理的（修了 / 删了）？\n(d) 修完后，论文里还有哪些数字是\"暂时找不到出处但保留\"的？为什么？\n\n---\n\n这次检查不是走过场。reviewer 拿到论文后只要随便挑一个数字让你说出处，你都得说得出来。请认真做，做完汇报。"

[飞书用户 用户]: 📊 论文自检任务（重大）：全文数据真实性与可追溯性彻底审计

这是一次彻底的大检查，必须认真对待。AI 写论文最致命的两个问题：编数字（hallucinate）和抄数字（数字之间不一致或来源不明）。这次的目标是把每一个出现在论文里的数字都"对账"清楚。

⚠️ 凡是查不到出处的数字，要么找到出处，要么删掉。不允许凭印象、凭"差不多"、凭老版本残留的数字留在论文里。

---

审计范围（无遗漏）：

1. 图（Figures）
   - 每张图里的数据点、bar 高度、曲线值、error bar
   - 图的 caption 里出现的数字（"reduces by 23%"、"on average 4.2"）
   - 坐标轴 tick 标注

2. 表（Tables）
   - 主结果表、ablation 表、超参表、基线表
   - 单元格里的每个数字，包括精度（小数位数）
   - 表头里的尺寸说明（"N=1000"、"k=5"）

3. 正文（Body）
   - Abstract 里的关键 claim 数字
   - Introduction 的 contribution 数字
   - Method 描述里的超参（learning rate、batch、steps、模型尺寸、参数量）
   - Experimental setup（数据集大小、训练 GPU 小时数、wall-clock 时间）
   - Analysis 段落里逐一引用的数字
   - Conclusion 里复述的数字

4. 附录（Appendix）
   - 完整的超参表、额外实验结果
   - 数据集详细统计
   - 失败案例的统计
   - Proof 里的常数、bound

---

对每个数字，逐个确认这 3 个问题：

Q1（真实性）：这个数字是从一次 真实运行 的实验里出来的吗？

合格证据：
- 在 results.yaml / 实验日志 / WandB run 里能定位到原始记录
- registry 里有对应的实验 ID
- 知道是 seed N 跑的、还是 mean of N seeds

不合格：
❌ "之前跑过一版差不多是这个数"
❌ "我们估算的"（除非论文明确说明 "estimated"）
❌ "上次 AI 写的时候放进来的，我也不知道哪来的"
❌ "占位符忘删了"

Q2（正确性）：抄到论文里有没有抄错？

- 原始记录 73.18% 抄到表里有没有变成 73.81% 或 73.2%？
- 小数位精度跟原始一致吗？（不要四舍五入到误差范围以外）
- 单位对吗（accuracy vs F1，pp vs %，BLEU vs ROUGE）

Q3（可追溯性）：数字写在论文里几个地方，每个地方都一致吗？

同一个指标可能出现在：Abstract、Introduction contribution、Method、Main Table、Analysis 段落、Conclusion —— 必须逐字逐位一致。不允许 Abstract 说 73.2%、表里 73.18%、Conclusion 又变成 73%。

---

审计步骤（请按这个顺序）：

1. 列清单：把论文里出现的所有数字（图+表+正文+附录）列出来。Director 自己估算一下总共多少个数字（一般 200-500 个）。

2. 逐个对账：每个数字找 results.yaml / wandb / 日志 / registry 里的源头。能找到就在自检表里记一行 "数字 X → 来源 Y"。

3. 不一致清单：列出 (a) 找不到出处的，(b) 跟源头对不上的，(c) 论文内部前后不一致的。

4. 决策：每个不一致项决定怎么修：
   - 能找到出处 → 改成正确的，并保证全文一致
   - 实在找不到出处 → 删掉，不要硬撑

5. 二次扫描：修完后再过一遍正文，确认没有遗留。

---

汇报内容（完成后）：

(a) 总共审计了多少个数字？
(b) 发现多少个"不一致 / 找不到出处"的问题？分别在哪些章节？
(c) 这些问题分别是怎么处理的（修了 / 删了）？
(d) 修完后，论文里还有哪些数字是"暂时找不到出处但保留"的？为什么？

---

这次检查不是走过场。reviewer 拿到论文后只要随便挑一个数字让你说出处，你都得说得出来。请认真做，做完汇报。

```text
Let me cross-reference Llama DocRED data:

**Llama-3.1-8B DocRED Unconstrained** (w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval):
- DR: Paper=0.49%, Registry=DR_pct=0.49 ✓ (note: registry uses different format here — DR_pct=0.49 means 0.49%)
- CF: Paper=0.08, Registry=... hmm, I don't see CF in the metrics. Let me check. The registry shows RV_pct=0.15 but no CF. 

Looking at the actual metrics in the registry, I'm seeing percentage values listed directly rather than as fractions, so DR_pct=0.49 represents 0.49%. But there's no CF metric showing up — the paper reports CF=0.08%, yet I can't locate where that comes from in these metrics. It seems different experiments are using different metric formats, which is making the comparison tricky.

I'm wondering if the CF value might be coming from a separate analysis or bootstrap confidence interval results, since the unconstrained mode probably doesn't track CF violations by default anyway — those are specifically about conflicting entity types across triples, which wouldn't apply when constraints aren't being enforced.

Now checking the constrained Llama-3.1-8B DocRED results: the domain range violation rate matches at 0.21%, and I'm verifying that the registry's dr_violation_rate of 0.21 is indeed in percentage form (33 violations out of 16074 relations = 0.21%). The CF, R-F1, and E-F1 metrics all align perfectly with what the paper reports.

For the improvement metric, the 57% reduction in domain range violations checks out mathematically. I still need to pull the Qwen DocRED numbers and track down that missing CF value for the unconstrained Llama setup, since the registry seems to use inconsistent naming conventions across different experiment types.
```

        "content": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Figure: Cross-Model Heatmap (placed here for right-column [t] on p8)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_crossmodel_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{DR reduction (\\%) across 3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets (zero-shot S1). Calibrated models (Qwen, Llama) achieve $\\geq$90\\% on SciERC; DocRED's higher $\\bar{\\kappa}$ reduces effectiveness. Full violation breakdown in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model} (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).}\n\\label{fig:crossmodel-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nNeither layer alone suffices: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%, an enabling dependency that weakens at scale (84\\% at 70B).\nThis non-additive interaction arises because the grammar layer provides a structured prerequisite: by restricting relation tokens to $\\mathcal{R}$ and entity type tokens to $\\mathcal{E}$, it ensures the semantic layer receives well-formed inputs on which domain/range lookups can operate.\nWithout grammar-enforced vocabulary membership, the semantic layer cannot reliably identify which relation governs a given type position.\n\nOntoCoDec composes both layers, reducing DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across model families and datasets ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp R-F1).\nThe residual DR$\\to$CF conversion is a severity downgrade rather than an error introduction: DR violations produce ontology-invalid triples that corrupt downstream knowledge graphs and require re-extraction, while CF violations (cross-triple type inconsistency) preserve individually valid triples and are resolvable via beam search or post-hoc reranking.\nBeam $k{=}4$ achieves near-zero violations at 8B, making generative IE reliably schema-compliant.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }

under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-latency}).\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\n% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep\n\\emph{Can beam search mitigate CF violations?}\nWith LoRA, beam search~\\cite{anderson2017guided} achieves near-zero violations at 8B on both SciERC and DocRED (DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\\% for Llama-3.1-8B; $\\leq$0.01\\% residual CF for Qwen3-8B); at 70B, beam $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$RV${=}$ET${=}$0\\% with one residual CF on SciERC (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nA beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, at 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% at 1.39$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:beam-pareto}; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nThis yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\%, DR${\\leq}$0.21\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\\times$, near-zero violations at 8B).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: Fine-tuning composability (moved here for page 8 placement)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$; power analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:power-analysis-full}).\nE-F1 (entity F1) includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF (parse failure): 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B-class validation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\n\\emph{Does the layer interaction change with model scale?}\nOn Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%); semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction vs.\\ 8B's 24\\% (Figure~\\ref{fig:scale-dependency}).\nLoRA confirms composability: DR\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% on both datasets (R-F1 44--45; Appendices~\\ref{app:70b-ablation},~\\ref{app:70b-docred-zs},~\\ref{app:70b-docred},~\\ref{app:quantization}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{GCD comparison.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only row (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}) is analytically equivalent to GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}/BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms this (DR${=}$15.26\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nThe ${\\sim}$15\\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\\% total reduction).\n",

        "content": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, $+$10\\% latency) and beam S1 (near-zero violations, 1.39$\\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (relation F1 (R-F1) 40--46\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 development documents; test annotations are not publicly available; 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a standalone ontology specification); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.\nOur R-F1 range (40--46\\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\\%~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.\nFor reference, the supervised end-to-end SOTA on SciERC achieves 41.6\\% strict Rel-F1 (PL-Marker~\\cite{ye2022packed}) and 36.8\\% (PURE~\\cite{zhong2021frustratingly}); while evaluation protocols differ, our 40--46\\% R-F1 range under LoRA falls within a comparable band.\nDocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nPrompt templates and reproducibility details (hyperparameters, dataset statistics) in Appendices~\\ref{app:prompts} and~\\ref{app:reproducibility}.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n\n% Figure 6: Violation composition (placed before figure* to land on page 6)\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_violation_composition.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation composition across constraint configurations (SciERC, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot). As layers compose, DR (irrecoverable) drops 94\\% while CF (correctable) becomes the sole residual, a severity downgrade from ontology-invalid to cross-triple type inconsistency.}\n\\label{fig:violation-composition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n% Figures 4+5 side-by-side (spanning both columns)\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/fig2_per_relation_dr.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-relation DR violation rates on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, unconstrained). Rates correlate with constraint tightness $\\kappa(r)$. Llama-3.1-8B shows similar patterns (Appendix~\\ref{app:llama-per-relation}).}\n  \\label{fig:per_relation_dr}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/fig_scale_dependency.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer's independent DR reduction grows from 24\\% at 8B to 84\\% at 70B, reducing reliance on grammar-enforced vocabulary.}\n  \\label{fig:scale-dependency}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\end{figure*}\n",

Tot.\\ = DR + CF; bold = best (CF excluded).\nF1 (\\%). S1/S2 = single/two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nGrammar = grammar-constrained decoding (GCD) ceiling~\\cite{geng2023gcd}.\n$\\dagger$vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\nDeterministic (greedy, $T{=}$0); robustness evidence in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model} and Appendices~\\ref{app:bootstrap-effect-sizes},~\\ref{app:prompt-sensitivity},~\\ref{app:doc-length},~\\ref{app:sampling}.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Zero-shot diagnostic (compressed)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n\\emph{Does grammar-constrained decoding alone resolve DR violations?}\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} establishes that DR violations are pervasive (15--18\\% across datasets) and that constraint layers interact non-additively: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%, the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}.\nOn DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing out-of-vocabulary (OOV)$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendices~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs},~\\ref{app:docred-zs-ablation}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nThe DR-to-CF conversion is a severity downgrade, not an error introduction (Figure~\\ref{fig:violation-composition}): DR violations produce ontology-invalid triples that corrupt downstream knowledge graphs and cannot be fixed without re-extraction, while CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency) can be resolved via fine-tuning, beam search, or post-hoc reranking (TCR eliminates all CF with bounded DR regression; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nZero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.\n\n% Figure 6 (violation-composition) moved earlier in source for page 6 placement\n\n% Cross-model table is in §6.1 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning (expanded)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\n\\emph{Does OntoCoDec retain value after fine-tuning?}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 0.5--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}; full fine-tuning in Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (41--69\\% on SciERC, 2--75\\% on DocRED; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\%; Appendices~\\ref{app:cf-distribution},~\\ref{app:cascade-analysis}); beam ($k{=}4$) achieves near-zero violations at 8B (R-F1 43--46; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% CF analysis folded into composability paragraph above;       "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task.       "- statusline-setup: Use this agent to configure the user's Claude Code status line setting. (Tools: Read, Edit)"

Related Work}\\n2\\t\\\\label{app:related_work}\\n3\\t\\n4\\t% ===========================================================================\\n5\\t% Paradigm 1: Grammar-Constrained Decoding\\n6\\t% ===========================================================================\\n7\\t\\\\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}\\n8\\tRecent work has established efficient algorithms for restricting LLM outputs to conform to context-free grammars, making grammar-constrained decoding the de facto standard for obtaining structured output from language models.\\n9\\tXGrammar~\\\\cite{dong2025xgrammar} partitions the vocabulary into context-independent and context-dependent tokens and maintains persistent pushdown automata across decoding steps, achieving up to 100$\\\\times$ speedup over prior CFG-constrained decoding methods.\\n10\\tOutlines~\\\\cite{willard2023outlines} reformulates constrained generation as finite-state machine transitions with offline vocabulary indexing, which enables efficient enforcement of regular-expression and JSON-schema constraints without runtime overhead per token.\\n11\\tLMQL~\\\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql} introduces a declarative query language that compiles type, length, and regex constraints into token-level masks, providing a programmable interface for specifying multi-variable output structures.\\n12\\tThese systems ensure that every generated token belongs to a grammar-valid continuation, but they do not address the \\\\emph{distribution distortion} introduced by naive token masking.\\n13\\tA parallel line of work tackles this problem directly: DOMINO~\\\\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino} minimizes distributional divergence during CFG enforcement by formulating mask selection as an optimization problem, and ASAp~\\\\cite{park2024grammar} provides a provably unbiased sampling algorithm that preserves the original LM distribution under grammar constraints.\\n14\\tTogether, these advances have made grammar-constrained decoding both fast and statistically principled, and provide a robust foundation for structured generation.\\n15\\tHowever, all of these methods operate exclusively at the syntactic level: they ensure format validity (e.g., well-formed JSON, valid enum values) but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as ontology domain/range rules that depend on previously generated content.\\n16\\tThis syntactic-only limitation motivates OntoCoDec's semantic extension: our grammar layer builds on XGrammar for structural (JSON schema and enum) constraints, while a second semantic layer enforces relational type constraints that are inherently beyond the expressive power of context-free grammars.\\n17\\t\\n18\\t% ===========================================================================\\n19\\t% Paradigm 1.5: Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code\\n20\\t% ===========================================================================\\n21\\t\\\\paragraph{Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code.}\\n22\\tIn programming-language generation, constrained decoding has been extended beyond syntax to enforce semantic properties such as type correctness; this work demonstrates that richer-than-CFG constraints can be compiled into token-level filtering.\\n23\\tIn the text-to-SQL domain, PICARD~\\\\cite{scholak2021picard} maintains an incremental SQL parsing state that rejects tokens violating syntax or referencing non-existent database columns at each decoding step, achieving state-of-the-art results on Spider and CoSQL without modifying model weights.\\n24\\tWhile mechanistically similar to OntoCoDec's token-level filtering, PICARD operates over a \\\\emph{fixed, fully enumerated} schema (database table and column names known before decoding begins).\\n25\\tIn contrast, OntoCoDec's entity registry is populated \\\\emph{during} generation as entity spans are extracted from free text; constraint sets evolve with the decoding prefix, a fundamental distinction between querying a known schema and extracting from unstructured text.\\n26\\tSynchromesh~\\\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} pioneers Constrained Semantic Decoding (CSD), which enforces syntax, type, and scope constraints simultaneously during code generation from pre-trained LMs without fine-tuning; this establishes that semantic constraints can be expressed as dynamic token masks conditioned on the generation prefix.\\n27\\tMGD~\\\\cite{mgd_neurips2023} uses static-analysis monitors to perform incremental type checking at decode time; SantaCoder-1.1B with MGD outperforms text-davinci-003 on repository-level code completion by ensuring that every generated identifier is type-correct with respect to the surrounding code context.\\n28\\tMost recently, \\\\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile full type systems, including generic types and subtyping, into prefix automata for token-level logits masking and reduce compilation errors by over 50\\\\%.\\n29\\tThis last system is the closest mechanistic analog to OntoCoDec, applying the same ``type system $\\\\to$ automaton $\\\\to$ logits mask'' pipeline, though in a fundamentally different domain.\\n30\\tThe key technical difference lies in the constraint structure: programming-language type systems feature deep subtyping hierarchies and parametric polymorphism, whereas IE ontology axioms specify flat domain/range sets; a relation $R$ accepts head entities from set $\\\\mathcal{D}(R)$ and tail entities from set $\\\\mathcal{R}(R)$, with no subtype chains.\\n31\\tConversely, IE introduces a challenge absent in code generation: the constraint target (entity type) must be inferred from free-text spans rather than read from declared type annotations, so a stateful entity registry must track extracted types during autoregressive generation.\\n32\\tThese methods collectively demonstrate that semantic (type) constraints at decode time are both feasible and highly effective, but exclusively in the code domain where type systems are formally specified.\\n33\\tOntoCoDec is the first to \\\\emph{automatically compile} IE ontology axioms, including domain/range constraints, into token-level decoding masks for information extraction, migrating the type-constrained decoding paradigm from code generation to IE.\\n34\\t\\n35\\t% ===========================================================================\\n36\\t% Paradigm 2: Vocabulary/Type-Constrained Generation\\n37\\t% ===========================================================================\\n38\\t\\\\paragraph{Vocabulary and Type-Constrained Generation.}\\n39\\tThe idea of enforcing entity-type compatibility constraints for information extraction dates back to pre-neural methods.\\n40\\t\\\\citet{roth_yih_2004} formulate joint entity and relation identification as an integer linear program (ILP) that enforces type constraints (e.g., \\\\textsc{Emp-Org} only between \\\\textsc{Per} and \\\\textsc{Org} entities); this establishes the foundational principle that relational type compatibility should be a hard constraint, not a soft preference learned from data.\\n41\\tIn the autoregressive era, GENRE~\\\\cite{decao2021genre} pioneers trie-based constrained beam search for autoregressive entity retrieval, restricting each decoding step to tokens consistent with valid entity names from a predefined catalog.\\n42\\tGenIE~\\\\cite{josifoski2022genie} extends this approach to end-to-end information extraction with state-based constraints that enforce valid triple structures, achieving strong performance on closed-world knowledge base population, though it requires task-specific fine-tuning on the target schema.\\n43\\tAdaKGC~\\\\cite{adakgc_emnlp2023} introduces schema-constrained dynamic decoding with trie-based prefix trees that adapt to the target schema, enforcing that generated entity and relation names belong to a predefined inventory, a vocabulary-level constraint that ensures name validity but does not verify type compatibility between a relation and its arguments.\\n44\\tGCD~\\\\cite{geng2023gcd} provides a unified grammar-constrained decoding framework for structured NLP tasks including IE, introducing input-dependent grammars that adapt constraints based on the source text. GCD is the closest precursor to OntoCoDec in applying logits masking to IE, though limited to syntactic constraints.\\n45\\tNeuroLogic Decoding~\\\\cite{lu2021neurologic} expresses lexical inclusion and exclusion constraints as predicate logic formulas enforced during beam search, offering a general mechanism for token-level control that can encode vocabulary membership but not relational type rules.\\n46\\tThese approaches constrain \\\\emph{which tokens} can appear (vocabulary membership) but not \\\\emph{which type combinations} are valid for a given relation.\\n47\\tOntoCoDec's semantic layer enforces relational constraints (e.g., that the head entity of relation $R$ must belong to type set $\\\\mathcal{D}(R)$) which cannot be expressed as static vocabulary restrictions because the valid set depends on the relation type generated earlier in the same sequence.\\n48\\tWhile Roth \\\\& Yih's ILP formulation shares the same type-compatibility motivation, it operates as a post-hoc reranker over discrete candidates; OntoCoDec integrates enforcement directly into autoregressive decoding, preventing violations rather than filtering them.\\n49\\t\\n50\\t% ===========================================================================\\n51\\t% Paradigm 3: Structural Constraints for IE\\n52\\t% ===========================================================================\\n53\\t\\\\paragraph{Structural Constraints via Training and Prompting.}\\n54\\tAn orthogonal approach encodes IE output structure through model training or prompt design rather than decoding-time enforcement, relying on learned representations to produce schema-compliant outputs.\\n55\\tUIE~\\\\cite{lu2022uie} defines a Structured Extraction Language and trains a unified text-to-structure model across 13 IE datasets, relying on large-scale pre-training to internalize output format constraints.\\n56\\tGoLLIE~\\\\cite{sainz2024gollie} expresses annotation guidelines as Python dataclass definitions and fine-tunes Code-LLaMA to generate typed code, achieving strong zero-shot transfer to unseen IE schemas through the implicit type structure of programming languages.\\n57\\tKnowCoder~\\\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024} takes this further by encoding 29K entity types from Wikidata as a Python class hierarchy, training a Code-LLM to generate structured extraction code that implicitly respects type inheritance, the most comprehensive schema-as-code approach to date.\\n58\\tCodeIE~\\\\cite{li2023codeie} similarly reformulates IE as code generation, using the structural regularity of programming languages to reduce output format errors.\\n59\\tADELIE~\\\\cite{qi2024adelie} uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align LLMs on IE tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results among open-source models while preserving general capabilities, though compliance with ontological constraints remains a learned soft preference rather than a hard constraint.\\n60\\tIn the supervised setting, joint entity-relation architectures such as SpERT~\\\\cite{eberts2020spert} and PFN~\\\\cite{yan2021pfn} enforce type consistency implicitly through shared representations: SpERT classifies entity spans and relation pairs in a single BERT pass with span-level negative sampling, while PFN decomposes feature encoding into entity-specific, relation-specific, and shared partitions via gating mechanisms.\\n61\\tThese models achieve strong performance but are limited to fixed, predefined schemas and cannot generalize to unseen ontologies without retraining, precisely the ontology-portable scenario requiring no constraint-specific training that OntoCoDec targets.\\n62\\tEnriCo~\\\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies Answer Set Programming (ASP) constraints during decoding of joint entity-relation extraction models, enforcing structural type compatibility at generation time. This is a decoding-time approach, though",

Extract all entities and relations from the following document.} \\\\\\\\[4pt]\\n35\\t\\\\texttt{Entity types: PER, ORG, LOC, TIME, NUM, MISC} \\\\\\\\[4pt]\\n36\\t\\\\texttt{Relation types: country, instance of, performer, ...\\\\ (96 Wikidata relation types; full list omitted for space)} \\\\\\\\[4pt]\\n37\\t\\\\texttt{Output a JSON object with exactly this structure:} \\\\\\\\\\n38\\t\\\\texttt{\\\\{\\\"entities\\\": [\\\\{\\\"text\\\": \\\"entity name\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"ENTITY\\\\_TYPE\\\"\\\\}],} \\\\\\\\\\n39\\t\\\\texttt{\\\\ \\\"relations\\\": [\\\\{\\\"head\\\": \\\"head entity\\\", \\\"tail\\\": \\\"tail entity\\\", \\\"type\\\": \\\"relation name\\\",} \\\\\\\\\\n40\\t\\\\texttt{\\\\ \\\\ \\\"head\\\\_type\\\": \\\"ENTITY\\\\_TYPE\\\", \\\"tail\\\\_type\\\": \\\"ENTITY\\\\_TYPE\\\"\\\\}]\\\\}} \\\\\\\\[4pt]\\n41\\t\\\\texttt{Output ONLY the JSON, no other text.} \\\\\\\\\\n42\\t\\\\midrule\\n43\\t\\\\textbf{DocRED --- User Prompt} goon\"}\n{\"parentUuid\":\"2115e428-533f-49f0-8e8e-f0051d2c5ab1\",\"isSidechain\":true,\"promptId\":\"a8890410-35c9-4ceb-8603-c0bc8f1daf5c\",\"agentId\":\"a16b2408c4cfcd2a6\",\"type\":\"user\",\"message\":{\"role\":\"user\",\"content\":[{\"tool_use_id\":\"toolu_01QBNWRcJqSyRWyE89zoc8HS\",\"type\":\"tool_result\",\"content\":\"1\\t\\\\section{Extended Related Work}\\n2\\t\\\\label{app:related_work}\\n3\\t\\n4\\t% ===========================================================================\\n5\\t% Paradigm 1: Grammar-Constrained Decoding\\n6\\t% ===========================================================================\\n7\\t\\\\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}\\n8\\tRecent work has established efficient algorithms for restricting LLM outputs to conform to context-free grammars, making grammar-constrained decoding the de facto standard for obtaining structured output from language models.\\n9\\tXGrammar~\\\\cite{dong2025xgrammar} partitions the vocabulary into context-independent and context-dependent tokens and maintains persistent pushdown automata across decoding steps, achieving up to 100$\\\\times$ speedup over prior CFG-constrained decoding methods.\\n10\\tOutlines~\\\\cite{willard2023outlines} reformulates constrained generation as finite-state machine transitions with offline vocabulary indexing, which enables efficient enforcement of regular-expression and JSON-schema constraints without runtime overhead per token.\\n11\\tLMQL~\\\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql} introduces a declarative query language that compiles type, length, and regex constraints into token-level masks, providing a programmable interface for specifying multi-variable output structures.\\n12\\tThese systems ensure that every generated token belongs to a grammar-valid continuation, but they do not address the \\\\emph{distribution distortion} introduced by naive token masking.\\n13\\tA parallel line of work tackles this problem directly: DOMINO~\\\\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino} minimizes distributional divergence during CFG enforcement by formulating mask selection as an optimization problem, and ASAp~\\\\cite{park2024grammar} provides a provably unbiased sampling algorithm that preserves the original LM distribution under grammar constraints.\\n14\\tTogether, these advances have made grammar-constrained decoding both fast and statistically principled, and provide a robust foundation for structured generation.\\n15\\tHowever, all of these methods operate exclusively at the syntactic level: they ensure format validity (e.g., well-formed JSON, valid enum values) but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as ontology domain/range rules that depend on previously generated content.\\n16\\tThis syntactic-only limitation motivates OntoCoDec's semantic extension: our grammar layer builds on XGrammar for structural (JSON schema and enum) constraints, while a second semantic layer enforces relational type constraints that are inherently beyond the expressive power of context-free grammars.\\n17\\t\\n18\\t% ===========================================================================\\n19\\t% Paradigm 1.5: Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code\\n20\\t% ===========================================================================\\n21\\t\\\\paragraph{Semantic Constrained Decoding for Code.}\\n22\\tIn programming-language generation, constrained decoding has been extended beyond syntax to enforce semantic properties such as type correctness; this work demonstrates that richer-than-CFG constraints can be compiled into token-level filtering.\\n23\\tIn the text-to-SQL domain, PICARD~\\\\cite{scholak2021picard} maintains an incremental SQL parsing state that rejects tokens violating syntax or referencing non-existent database columns at each decoding step, achieving state-of-the-art results on Spider and CoSQL without modifying model weights.\\n24\\tWhile mechanistically similar to OntoCoDec's token-level filtering, PICARD operates over a \\\\emph{fixed, fully enumerated} schema (database table and column names known before decoding begins).\\n25\\tIn contrast, OntoCoDec's entity registry is populated \\\\emph{during} generation as entity spans are extracted from free text; constraint sets evolve with the decoding prefix, a fundamental distinction between querying a known schema and extracting from unstructured text.\\n26\\tSynchromesh~\\\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} pioneers Constrained Semantic Decoding (CSD), which enforces syntax, type, and scope constraints simultaneously during code generation from pre-trained LMs without fine-tuning; this establishes that semantic constraints can be expressed as dynamic token masks conditioned on the generation prefix.\\n27\\tMGD~\\\\cite{mgd_neurips2023} uses static-analysis monitors to perform incremental type checking at decode time; SantaCoder-1.1B with MGD outperforms text-davinci-003 on repository-level code completion by ensuring that every generated identifier is type-correct with respect to the surrounding code context.\\n28\\tMost recently, \\\\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile full type systems, including generic types and subtyping, into prefix automata for token-level logits masking and reduce compilation errors by over 50\\\\%.\\n29\\tThis last system is the closest mechanistic analog to OntoCoDec, applying the same ``type system $\\\\to$ automaton $\\\\to$ logits mask'' pipeline, though in a fundamentally different domain.\\n30\\tThe key technical difference lies in the constraint structure: programming-language type systems feature deep subtyping hierarchies and parametric polymorphism, whereas IE ontology axioms specify flat domain/range sets; a relation $R$ accepts head entities from set $\\\\mathcal{D}(R)$ and tail entities from set $\\\\mathcal{R}(R)$, with no subtype chains.\\n31\\tConversely, IE introduces a challenge absent in code generation: the constraint target (entity type) must be inferred from free-text spans rather than read from declared type annotations, so a stateful entity registry must track extracted types during autoregressive generation.\\n32\\tThese methods collectively demonstrate that semantic (type) constraints at decode time are both feasible and highly effective, but exclusively in the code domain where type systems are formally specified.\\n33\\tOntoCoDec is the first to \\\\emph{automatically compile} IE ontology axioms, including domain/range constraints, into token-level decoding masks for information extraction, migrating the type-constrained decoding paradigm from code generation to IE.\\n34\\t\\n35\\t% ===========================================================================\\n36\\t% Paradigm 2: Vocabulary/Type-Constrained Generation\\n37\\t% ===========================================================================\\n38\\t\\\\paragraph{Vocabulary and Type-Constrained Generation.}\\n39\\tThe idea of enforcing entity-type compatibility constraints for information extraction dates back to pre-neural methods.\\n40\\t\\\\citet{roth_yih_2004} formulate joint entity and relation identification as an integer linear program (ILP) that enforces type constraints (e.g., \\\\textsc{Emp-Org} only between \\\\textsc{Per} and \\\\textsc{Org} entities); this establishes the foundational principle that relational type compatibility should be a hard constraint, not a soft preference learned from data.\\n41\\tIn the autoregressive era, GENRE~\\\\cite{decao2021genre} pioneers trie-based constrained beam search for autoregressive entity retrieval, restricting each decoding step to tokens consistent with valid entity names from a predefined catalog.\\n42\\tGenIE~\\\\cite{josifoski2022genie} extends this approach to end-to-end information extraction with state-based constraints that enforce valid triple structures, achieving strong performance on closed-world knowledge base population, though it requires task-specific fine-tuning on the target schema.\\n43\\tAdaKGC~\\\\cite{adakgc_emnlp2023} introduces schema-constrained dynamic decoding with trie-based prefix trees that adapt to the target schema, enforcing that generated entity and relation names belong to a predefined inventory, a vocabulary-level constraint that ensures name validity but does not verify type compatibility between a relation and its arguments.\\n44\\tGCD~\\\\cite{geng2023gcd} provides a unified grammar-constrained decoding framework for structured NLP tasks including IE, introducing input-dependent grammars that adapt constraints based on the source text. GCD is the closest precursor to OntoCoDec in applying logits masking to IE, though limited to syntactic constraints.\\n45\\tNeuroLogic Decoding~\\\\cite{lu2021neurologic} expresses lexical inclusion and exclusion constraints as predicate logic formulas enforced during beam search, offering a general mechanism for token-level control that can encode vocabulary membership but not relational type rules.\\n46\\tThese approaches constrain \\\\emph{which tokens} can appear (vocabulary membership) but not \\\\emph{which type combinations} are valid for a given relation.\\n47\\tOntoCoDec's semantic layer enforces relational constraints (e.g., that the head entity of relation $R$ must belong to type set $\\\\mathcal{D}(R)$) which cannot be expressed as static vocabulary restrictions because the valid set depends on the relation type generated earlier in the same sequence.\\n48\\tWhile Roth \\\\& Yih's ILP formulation shares the same type-compatibility motivation, it operates as a post-hoc reranker over discrete candidates; OntoCoDec integrates enforcement directly into autoregressive decoding, preventing violations rather than filtering them.\\n49\\t\\n50\\t% ===========================================================================\\n51\\t% Paradigm 3: Structural Constraints for IE\\n52\\t% ===========================================================================\\n53\\t\\\\paragraph{Structural Constraints via Training and Prompting.}\\n54\\tAn orthogonal approach encodes IE output structure through model training or prompt design rather than decoding-time enforcement, relying on learned representations to produce schema-compliant outputs.\\n55\\tUIE~\\\\cite{lu2022uie} defines a Structured Extraction Language and trains a unified text-to-structure model across 13 IE datasets, relying on large-scale pre-training to internalize output format constraints.\\n56\\tGoLLIE~\\\\cite{sainz2024gollie} expresses annotation guidelines as Python dataclass definitions and fine-tunes Code-LLaMA to generate typed code, achieving strong zero-shot transfer to unseen IE schemas through the implicit type structure of programming languages.\\n57\\tKnowCoder~\\\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024} takes this further by encoding 29K entity types from Wikidata as a Python class hierarchy, training a Code-LLM to generate structured extraction code that implicitly respects type inheritance, the most comprehensive schema-as-code approach to date.\\n58\\tCodeIE~\\\\cite{li2023codeie} similarly reformulates IE as code generation, using the structural regularity of programming languages to reduce output format errors.\\n59\\tADELIE~\\\\cite{qi2024adelie} uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align LLMs on IE tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results among open-source models while preserving general capabilities, though compliance with ontological constraints remains a learned soft preference rather than a hard constraint.\\n60\\tIn the supervised setting, joint entity-relation architectures such as SpERT~\\\\cite{eberts2020spert} and PFN~\\\\cite{yan2021pfn} enforce type consistency implicitly through shared representations: SpERT classifies entity spans and relation pairs in a single BERT pass with span-level negative sampling, while PFN decomposes feature encoding into entity-specific, relation-specific, and shared partitions via gating mechanisms.\\n61\\tThese models achieve strong performance but are limited to fixed, predefined schemas and cannot generalize to unseen ontologies without retraining, precisely the ontology-portable scenario requiring no constraint-specific training that OntoCoDec targets.\\n62\\tEnriCo~\\\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies Answer Set Programming (ASP) constraints during decoding of joint entity-relation extraction models, enforcing structural type compatibility at generation time. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01At3fNJF8QnmAj2A95pYDwS`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for information extraction (IE)~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations} (DR): structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations are pervasive (15--18\% of triples under zero-shot decoding and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning) and correlate with schema restrictiveness rather than model scale.
9	At competitive quality, even low residual violation rates compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 74\% of predictions including true positives (Appendices~\ref{app:cross-model-zs},~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (CFGs) via XGrammar (JSON structure, vocabulary membership); the \emph{semantic layer} enforces type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to any autoregressive LM with logits access.
20	We validate across three model families, two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), and event extraction (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}), with 70B-class supporting evidence (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
21	Composed with LoRA, greedy decoding reduces DR to near-zero ($\leq$\,0.21\%) at R-F1 40--46\% ($\pm$0.3\,pp, $+$10\% latency), with 97.6--100\% of removed triples confirmed as false positives; beam ($k{=}4$) achieves near-zero violations at ${\sim}$1.5$\times$ latency.
22	DR enforcement converts irrecoverable ontology violations into cross-field type inconsistencies (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned), a severity downgrade amenable to post-hoc resolution.
23	
24	OntoCoDec is orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.
25	
26	Our contributions:
27	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
28	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework that reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\leq$\,0.21\%) without retraining or significant quality loss, showing that these type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1; \S\ref{sec:decidability}).
29	    \item An empirical finding of non-additive layer interaction: grammar-only reduces DR by 3\% and semantic-only by 24\%, yet their composition achieves 94\%, because grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.
30	    \item Supporting validation: scale-dependent interaction at 70B (semantic-only DR reduction grows from 24\% to 84\%; \S\ref{sec:70b-results}); beam $k{=}4$ achieves near-zero violations at 8B; event extraction generalization on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
31	\end{enumerate}
32	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NzA42JqdRYvtL3jM2Zg5kw`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples: entity types that violate the schema's domain/range constraints for the predicted relation.
3	These domain/range violations (DR) persist even after fine-tuning and cannot be resolved by grammar-constrained decoding, which lacks cross-field type awareness.
4	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec} (\textbf{Onto}logy-\textbf{Co}mpiled \textbf{Dec}oding), a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles IE ontologies into token-level masks: a grammar layer enforces structural validity; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime via stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	The two layers interact non-additively: grammar-only reduces DR by 3\% and semantic-only by 24\%, yet their composition achieves 94\%.
7	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning across three model families and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec reduces DR to $\leq$\,0.21\% with minimal F1 impact ($\pm$0.3\,pp), converting irrecoverable type errors into correctable cross-field inconsistencies; beam search ($k{=}4$) achieves near-zero violations at ${\sim}$1.5$\times$ latency.
8	Validation on a 70B model shows the semantic layer's independent effectiveness grows from 24\% to 84\% DR reduction with scale\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/schema_semantic_constrained_ie-FE20/}.}.
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```
